ParlaMint-EE_2019-11-13_U1	Let's keep going. Today's 11th item is the first reading of the draft law amending the Law on the Flag and Registers of Ships and the Income Tax Act and related amendments to other laws and amending the Income Tax Act. I would like to ask the Minister for Economic Affairs and Infrastructure, Taavi Aasa, to serve as a waitress.
ParlaMint-EE_2019-11-13_U2	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Happy Members of the Riigikogu! Hello, new day. I will introduce you to the draft law amending the flag law of the ship and the Ship Registers Act and the Income Tax Act and related amendments to other laws and the Income Tax Act. Today we are in a situation where Estonia is in the leading position in the Baltic Sea for passenger ships and several large passenger ships fly the flag of Estonia, but unfortunately there are no major cargo ships flying the flag of Estonia. The last cargo ship left the Estonian flag in 2014. This bill is an important first step and is part of a comprehensive package of maritime sector development measures aimed at restoring the fleet flying the flag of Estonia, increasing the Estonian shore sector and, together, restoring Estonia's international reputation as a maritime state. In order to bring ships under the Estonian flag, the principles of taxation of seafarers and shipping companies must be amended, following the example of other European countries, and the registration of ships in the Estonian register of chartered ships without a crew made easier. At the same time, it is planned to create modern and customer-friendly maritime information systems and a functioning and efficient customer service working 24/7 at the Maritime Administration. The target group of the tax changes to be introduced by the draft is shipping companies operating vessels of 500 gross tonnage or more on international voyages or passenger ships engaged on regular voyages outside the European Economic Area. The European Commission's Guidelines on State aid to maritime transport apply only to maritime transport, i.e. the carriage of goods and passengers by sea, which means that tax changes cannot be applied to fishing vessels, which are not maritime transport. The draft proposes a number of tax changes. Since tax incentives constitute State aid, the draft has been drawn up in the light of the European Union guidelines on State aid in this field. In order to implement tax changes, Estonia first needs to obtain authorisation to grant State aid from the European Commission. Next, I will look at the tax changes that will be in force. In the case of seafarers employed on merchant ships of 500 gross tonnage or more engaged on international voyages or on passenger ships engaged on regular voyages outside the European Economic Area, bearing the flag of another country of the European Economic Area, the labour tax shall be calculated on the basis of a fixed tax instead of their actual remuneration. This means that regardless of the person's actual remuneration, his or her remuneration will be taxed up to a maximum of EUR 750 per specific amount provided for by law. The social tax is paid at a reduced rate for the seafarer, i.e. 20% of the tax base instead of the usual 33%, and the seafarers have the possibility to use voluntary health insurance. In addition to social tax, the draft provides for the calculation of unemployment and compulsory funded pension contributions on the basis of the tax base, i.e. EUR 750, rather than the actual salary of the seafarer. Shipping companies will be subject to a special corporate tax regime, i.e. a tonnage regime, which can be used as an alternative to the current tax system. More specifically, shipping companies will be able to implement either the current system by paying income tax on the company's distributed profits or apply the tonnage tax scheme. In the tonnage regime, the amount of taxable income generated by an undertaking's international commercial shipping shall be calculated as the product of the net tonnage of the vessel at its disposal and the ratios established. Therefore, more ships are expected in the Estonian ship register, but it will certainly be closely monitored to ensure that only ships that are technically in good condition and meet international requirements are included in the register of Estonia, and that comply with international regulations and requirements in the fields of social protection and safety of seafarers, maritime safety of the ship and the environment. All these requirements are still being monitored by the Estonian state, but in the future, keeping the ship in the register will become directly dependent on the compliance of the ship with international regulations. As has been said, shipping covers not only ships flying the flag of the state, but also a strong and sustainable shipping shore sector. According to a pan-European survey carried out in 2014, one job on a ship generates an additional 3.8 jobs on the shore. Shipping is the fourth largest in Europe in terms of productivity and above other modes of transport, such as air transport and land transport. I would ask for your support for this bill. Thank you.
ParlaMint-EE_2019-11-13_U3	Thank you, Minister. You have questions too. Peter Ernits, please!

Today, the deadline has been postponed to mid-December. However, the procedure has gone on in such a way that it can really be expected to obtain authorisation in mid-December. As regards whether the ships will come under the flag of Estonia after the adoption of the bill and its entry into force by the middle of next year, we hope they will. We'll do everything we can for this. But first of all, this bill is still the basis for the fact that it would be conceivable for ships to come back under the Estonian flag. If we do not adopt this law in this form, there is no presumption either.

ParlaMint-EE_2019-11-13_U18	We have reached today's last item on the agenda, which is the first reading of the draft law 103 on the amendment of consumer protection law initiated by the Government of the Republic and the related amendment of other laws. Mr Aasa, Minister for Economic Affairs and Infrastructure, would you like the floor to be a waitress?
ParlaMint-EE_2019-11-13_U19	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Honourable Members of the Riigikogu! I will introduce you to the draft law amending the Consumer Protection Act and related amendments to other laws. The aim of the draft is to enhance the protection of consumers' economic interests in the digital environment and, in particular, to implement Regulation 2017/2394 of the European Parliament and of the Council, which regulates cooperation between the competent authorities of the Member States in the detection and termination of cross-border infringements of consumer protection requirements. Today, consumers are doing a large part of their online transactions and the number of online shopping sites is constantly increasing. The sites are simultaneously accessible to a large number of consumers, regardless of their geographical location. For example, there are more than 5,000 e-traders today in Estonia alone and 119 million euros of e-purchases were made in August of this year. The volume of e-purchases made by Estonian consumers both in Estonia and abroad is constantly increasing. However, it is the case that not all traders comply with consumer protection requirements. More up-to-date measures are needed to quickly detect and eliminate infringements in the digital environment. The European Union regulation obliges Member States to grant minimum rights to competent authorities in order to ensure compliance with legislation protecting consumers' interests and effective protection of consumers' economic interests. In Estonia, the main competent authority is the Consumer Protection and Technical Surveillance Authority, which is responsible for monitoring consumer protection requirements. Today, the Consumer Protection and Technical Surveillance Authority is able to exercise a number of rights under the EU Regulation under national law. The amendment to the Consumer Protection Act now gives TTJA the right to ask and receive the information necessary to identify infringements from any person, including the credit institution and the communication service provider. In the digital environment, it is not always possible to understand who is behind the website or mobile app and information from third parties is needed to identify it. As a breach of consumer protection rules in the digital environment could cause economic damage to a large number of consumers, it is important to put an end to the infringement quickly in order to prevent it. In the event that the infringer's identity cannot be identified or contacted, the TTJA shall be given the right to take measures to restrict access to the website related to the infringement or to display warnings to consumers. Apart from the inclusion of the provisions necessary for the implementation of the EU Regulation, the Consumer Law also proposes amendments to simplify the rules of procedure of the Consumer Disputes Committee. On the basis of the current law, the Commission settles disputes between consumers and businesses within a three-member group. The amendment will allow the procedure to be carried out only with the participation of the chairman of the committee to be dealt with more quickly if the facts of the dispute are clear and similar disputes have already been settled by the Commission. The amendments made to the Financial Supervision Authority Act, the Credit Institutions Act, the Code of Misdemeanour Procedure and the Electronic Communications Act relate to the right of TTJA to receive information from third parties necessary to identify or eliminate the infringement. The amendment of the Aviation Act introduces sanctions for infringements of European Union regulations on air passenger rights. The amendments to the Tourism Act make it easier for companies providing travel management services who only offer or mediate travel services provided in Estonia. Under the current law, an undertaking providing a linked travel arrangement must have a guarantee of at least EUR 13000 in order to be able to meet its obligations towards consumers in the event of its insolvency. At the same time, there is no distinction as to whether the travel service is provided only in Estonia or abroad. However, according to the proposed change, if this service is provided only in Estonia, the guarantee may also be lower. The amendment to the Law of Obligations Act clarifies the provision which limits the demand for payment from the consumer when the consumer calls the telephone number given by the trader in connection with the contract concluded. It is stated more clearly that in such a case, the consumer will not pay more than the basic tariff fee for the telephone call. This means that the trader must not use a short number with a charge higher than the basic tariff for customer service. The draft is planned to enter into force on 17 January, as the European Union regulation will also apply from that date. I would like to ask you to support the bill.

Dear Minister, It has happened that, at the request of the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications, on 4 November, during the second reading of the Maritime Safety Act, the Economic Commission urgently included in the draft law amendments to the railway law extending for a new five-year period the derogations to the obligations imposed on railway undertakings by the European Community Regulation. Unfortunately, in the Committee on Economic Affairs, after the second reading of the Maritime Safety Act and the adoption of decisions, it became clear that the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications regrettably, in the preparation of the new railway law and in the preparation of the extension of derogations, the target group to which these amendments have a significant impact has been excluded. These are disabled people and their representative organisation, the Estonian Chamber of Disabled People. I am very pleased that yesterday, just yesterday afternoon, just before today's final vote on the Maritime Safety Act, the parties, i.e. the Committee on Economic Affairs, the Ministry of Economic Affairs and the CEO of the Estonian Chamber of Disabled People, finally met in the Committee on Economic Affairs. ParlaMint-EE_2019-11-13_U112	Honourable Minister of Finance! The Estonian capital market has been highly banking-driven. Companies finance their investments mainly through bank loans. And as a rule, banks do not offer equity investments. But pension funds do. Pension funds also offer longer investments than banks do. But so far the investments of banks' pension funds in the Estonian economy have been very modest. The change now took place because the previous composition of the Riigikogu adopted an amendment to the Pensions Act, which eased the rules of investment in Estonia and reduced these restrictions. At the same time, the reform of the second pension pillar you have initiated has reversed this positive development. Investment in Estonia by pension funds has started to decline and the limitation of investments has been reported. LHV Pank closed only the pension fund that invested in Estonia, merged it with another fund. SEB said that the pension reform will put an end to Estonian investments. Luminor Bank says that they will not make new illiquid investments in the local economy in the future, or minimally. Very undesirable developments! But I ask: how, as the initiator of your draft pension reform, are you satisfied with their first results and does this development meet the objectives set by the Pillar Reformers?
ParlaMint-EE_2019-11-13_U113	Thank you. It is true that I, too, was in the Committee on Monetary Affairs at the time and voted in favour of the same bill, even though it was a draft from the government that changed the conditions for investment. Broadly speaking, while in 2016 investments in the Estonian economy of pension funds were between 150 and 200 million, they are now approaching 600 million and this is undoubtedly a positive development. But I would point out that this fund, which you said it was closed, wasn't really closed, but it was merged. And this has also been done in Estonia before that funds are reorganized to achieve better returns. I agree that one of the most important economic risks of this whole second pillar pension reform is not so much the ability of banks and funds to cope. There is no doubt about that. The Financial Supervision Authority has carried out stress tests and says that there is basically no risk that our pension funds will become insolvent or that their'share' price will be significantly reduced as a result of pension reform. But this negative effect could occur. That's a risk we have to take into account. And against this risk, we must look for either a cure or a relief through the fact that, in the interests of liquidity management, pension funds must review their investments in real investments within Estonia. This is a concern and has been opposed by various proposals from market participants themselves during the preparation of the draft pension reform. I think that, in order to ensure liquidity, we have a number of compensatory or risk-relieving measures in this draft. There are also those proposals that were received in the public circle after the publication of the draft. We will no longer put them in the bill when we go to the government, but they will definitely come to the Riigikogu for consideration and I think we are very open to them. So we're aware of the risks. But first, the volume of these risks is quite small in view of the entire pension fund and the Estonian capital market, so they do not pose any systemic risk. And secondly, we've been looking for ways to mitigate the risks, and I think we've found them too.

ParlaMint-EE_2019-11-13_U116	Thank you. I don't think we've taken a passive and carefree attitude. Whether these behaviours are positive in the short term or in the long term, I think we can argue about this for a very long time. But the concern you are expressing is that all things have to be the same for the rest of their lives, because then there is no risk of working in the economy, but that is not the way it works. Now, speaking of measures, one concrete measure that we have put into this draft is a ten-year 'freeze period'. If a person joins a pension fund or leaves a fund, he or she cannot re-decide for ten years. This is precisely so that there is less commutation and less volatility. Another concrete measure, which has not been agreed, but which we are seriously considering, is an increase in the ceiling on the liquidity of the funds. It's 10% now. We'll discuss whether to double it or raise it a little bit more. This clearly takes off the risk that we will have a liquidity problem in the funds. But the rest is, of course, the choice of an investment strategy and it remains up to investors.

At the age of 14, 17% of boys and 14% of girls were drunk for the first time. At least once, 38% of school students aged 15 to 16 have used a drug in their lifetime. In the state budget for the coming year, no EUR 390000 is needed for the professional year of clinical psychologists. The Labour Force Study OSKA says that 14 new clinical psychologists are needed each year for the labour market. The Ministry of Social Affairs also admits that yes, we have too few clinical psychology. There is no national training system in Estonia how to obtain a clinical psychologist's qualification. Getting and studying for this depends on the employer of the psychologist and his own personal initiative, with no public support. In the budget for the coming year, there will also be no EUR 127000 for the implementation of the VEPA behavioural skills game in schools. VEPA is currently the only methodology that reduces risk behaviour and hyperactivity of children, based on research carried out in both the world and Estonia, and increases the willingness, friendliness and caringness of children. Applying methodology prevents drug use and later suicidal behaviour, and it also improves teacher class management and guidance skills. In the last five years, EUR 1 million of European Union money has been invested in the implementation of this research-based prevention programme. If the country does not find any additional funding for the implementation of this programme next year, there is a risk that these knowledge and skills will simply fade away and that students and teachers will be in difficulty at school. In the budget for the coming year, there will also be no EUR 112000 for the development of a children's helpline, to which children and their spotters are increasingly in need from year to year. Last year, 6500 visits were made to the children's helpline, and the statistics for the first half of this year show that almost 5,000 cases have already been received. The number of visitors is increasing, so the number of children receiving assistance is increasing. That could be the purpose of all of us. The Estonian state is rich enough to allow free public transport for everyone, which has brought the new concept of bus groups into the lexika. We are rich enough to celebrate the 100th anniversary of our dignified constitution with EUR 650000. We're rich enough to split up 4 million euros of roofing money among the MPs. But we do not have EUR 850000 to provide clinical psychologists and school psychologists with the necessary training to ensure a science-based prevention programme to protect children's mental health and develop a child helpline, which makes children who are increasingly in need of help get real support. It is not a question of lack of money; it is a question of priorities. As the Chairman of the Committee on Monetary Affairs said: every budget is the face of a government. Together with my colleagues Riina Sikkut, Helmen Küti and Signe Riisalo, I have brought before you four amendments which would create the assumption that children in need will receive help in the right time and as efficiently as possible. I ask for your support in voting on these amendments so that no life will be left unlived, so that no young life will be left unlived. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2019-11-13_U308	Honourable Vice-Chairman of the Riigikogu! Ladies and gentlemen, I would like to speak today on a very important subject, which I believe has not been given enough attention in society, also by politicians. There will also be no indication of this important problem or issue in the draft budget for next year. At one point, all of us may face the fact that we or someone close to us has suffered a serious illness that cannot be healed. This is when heaven and earth change places. This is a situation where, in addition to a doctor or nurse who treats illness and relieves physical distress, other specialists are also needed: psychologists, mental health care providers, social workers, experience counsellors who support the person and help him to understand his situation and cope with the difficult situation. In other parts of the world, such patients are dealt with by professionals who work as a team and provide comprehensive support to the patient. Northern Estonia Regional Hospital has built the only palliative treatment centre in Estonia for just over three years. Unfortunately, the health insurance fund only finances the work of nurses and doctors, and all other specialists must pay the hospital from their own funds. The hospital budget, however, has its limits. This situation is also characterised by the fact that the palliative treatment centre in PERH is the only one in Estonia to date. In PERH, the palliative treatment centre also works mainly because of the enthusiasm and a high level of empathy of the specialists working there. I can say this with certainty because I also had the opportunity to work two and a half years as a psychologist at the Palliative Treatment Centre of the North Estonian Regional Hospital. Against this background, I, together with three of my colleagues, Kalle Laanet, Andrus Seed and Signe Riisalo, tabled an amendment to the state budget for the following year, where, if adopted, the support specialists needed for palliative treatment could be financed and the establishment of a palliative treatment service could be established elsewhere in Estonia. Please, ladies and gentlemen, think that we may all need palliative care at some point, and support Amendment 30. Thank you!

ParlaMint-EE_2019-11-13_U310	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, On 11 October, the Estonian Chamber of Commerce and Industry, together with 11 representative business organisations, called on the Finance Committee of the Riigikogu in connection with the decision of the Government of the Republic of 24 September to direct nearly 39 million euros from the resource efficiency measure to other areas. Estonia's resource efficiency is the last in the European Union, and without state support, the situation may not improve rapidly. Mining and processing companies may fail to invest at least EUR 111 million to improve resource efficiency. The state receives millions of euros of tax revenue from investments and added value. Several international organisations, such as the European Commission and the International Energy Agency, have recently advised Estonia to channel more money into promoting resource efficiency and the circular economy. Business representative organisations have proposed: if it is not possible for the country to return some 39 million to the resource efficiency measure, a refund of EUR 22 million is considered essential, allowing all companies that have already done the necessary preparatory work in the form of resource audits or who are still in the process of carrying out this work. I believe that on such important issues as increasing the resource efficiency of companies and supporting our business environment, increasing competitiveness and the environment, the government cannot and must not act in a word-breaking manner, all the more so as the basic principles of the Central Party, the Conservative People's Party of Estonia and the Association of Government of the Isamaa Party for the years 2019 to 2023 state that the Government of the Republic will continue to support investments in the resource efficiency of companies. As a result, I proposed a revision of the draft state budget for 2020 to channel EUR 25 million into the resource efficiency measure so that at least those companies that have carried out a mandatory resource audit as a precondition for the application could apply for investment support. I would ask you, ladies and gentlemen, to take our business request seriously and support my proposal for improvement. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2019-11-13_U312	Mr President-in-Office of the Council. Ladies and gentlemen! Today, at second reading, the Riigikogu has almost the most important draft of the year, the state budget for 2020. When almost nine months ago the elections were held and people were asked for support to implement the promises, political parties prioritised increased funding for research and development, reduction of regional disparities, support for agriculture, improved livelihoods of Estonian people and families, increased care allowance and reduced care burden, and retirement for the elderly. One of the most generous promises was the political parties that formed today's government. People gave their votes, believed in the promiseers, and now they are waiting for their promises to be redeemed. Unfortunately, the budget speaks a different language, and it does not make any sense of what was promised with a big mouth. The Social Democrats have tabled 12 amendments to the draft state budget for 2020 and are committed to making the place more accessible to those in need in the nursing home, to the full payment of transitional support to farmers, and to research and higher education so as to receive the necessary additional funding. The proposals presented are realistic, covered by suitable sources of cover and improve the situation of those who have been deceived and failed by the government. Estonia's social protection system is characterised by a small share of social protection expenditure in GDP compared to other EU countries. The same applies to old-age pensions: Estonia's expenditure on old-age pensions is one of the lowest in Europe. Unfortunately, today's government also violates this principle, which has been in Estonia for years, that the average pension is exempt from income tax. In 2020, all pension beneficiaries will pay income tax on an amount exceeding EUR 500. At the same time, the own contribution to paying for maintenance costs in Estonia is one of the highest in Europe. The trend is, to the detriment of the person in need of care and his/her loved ones, i.e. in Estonia, care is largely the responsibility of carers or finance. The burden on carers is very high, both mentally and financially, and the family as a whole is disturbed, not to mention the different treatment of the elderly without children or children. In recent years, unfortunately, the financial contribution of local authorities has decreased and public expenditure on general care has increased. For example, the share of the elderly or his family in paying for the care home was 65% in 2010, it had already risen to 76% in 2016 and it was 78% in 2017. Abandoning the work of family members and becoming a carer if the difference between the income of a family member in need of care and the cost of a nursing home cannot be paid, it is certainly not the best solution for both people and the state. A professional career will not be carried out, the state will not pay taxes, the children you want will not be born. So far, no funds have been found in the state budget to support people with care needs and their families in a comparable volume to those of other countries. Such support is not only an additional cost for the public sector, but also the tax revenue that people who have cared for their loved ones at home are going to bring back to the labour market when they return to the state budget. Our amendment to allocate EUR 20 million to the welfare programme would significantly alleviate the problem of the high cost of a nursing home in the new year. We see that, as of 1 July, care homes for people in need of assistance and care would be guaranteed, if necessary, for pensions, which would mean that the state had to cover the price difference with the municipalities. This obligation to pay from both the state and local government budgets makes municipalities create all previous social services that guarantee long-term and supported livelihoods for the elderly at home. As a result, the number of people who may need care would be reduced, but if there were suitable support and assistance, you could also manage at home today and in the future. For its part, the state would eventually start to look at the health and care system as a whole and united and a weak link between the various services and levels could be resolved. People working in social and health systems have long understood the crucial importance of introducing integrated care and health care and their integrated financing. In short, every person gets the right service at the right time. This is the only way to ensure that people live in their homes for as long as possible and that care is needed only when there is a very high need for help. In 1990, Pope John Paul II concluded his century letter as follows: "... never was the decisive factor in the production of the land and later it was the capital... Today, the decisive factor is the person himself. " Let us keep our people, and let us take its first step together! A good start would be to support Socialist Amendment 27, which would significantly alleviate the problem of high cost of nursing homes as early as 1 July 2020. In order to allow the Committee on Monetary Affairs to examine, analyse and support both this proposal and other good amendments in a calm manner, the Social Democrats also propose that the second reading of the draft State Budget Act 2020 82 be interrupted. Thank you.

We have more people, more countries, more choices, more goods, more services. We also came up with an e-residency program that is envied elsewhere in the world. If you want to change your driving licence, you can do it very easily on the Internet. But we also have bad examples if you have to click and click, but you won't find anything. It's especially difficult for older people. These systems are too complex and people are frustrated by the fact that they have to submit their data several times, over and over again. Why do I have to submit everything again once I've submitted my data to the state? There were different ideas in the programmes of many political parties, perhaps even all political parties, about how to cut red tape. Bureaucracy can certainly be reduced by e-services being easier to manage and better understood by people. But like I said, it all takes money. Money is also taken so that people can consume these services at all. It is in rural areas that it is also necessary to build internet connections so that people can access all these services. These services cannot be developed unless additional funds are allocated for this purpose. We must not rest on the laurels that we have received because we have developed an e-state and are therefore known in the world. Others make similar solutions and even pass us by. We need new success stories and we need to move our services to a new level. A total of 25 million are intended to complement and develop IT services throughout the state budget. This is not enough, given the size of the country and how important it is to people. So our proposal, which I also ask colleagues from other political groups in the Riigikogu to support, concerns the allocation of additional money for IT investments. Of course, we are not the ones who have to say exactly where this money has to go, but without this money being allocated, without agreeing that it really is necessary, we cannot go on. IT services will not be reduced, they will be more, they must be accessible to people, convenient and easy to use. I therefore ask, ladies and gentlemen, to support Amendment 28. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2019-11-13_U346	Good manager! Ladies and gentlemen, First of all, I would like to thank the opposition for its good and sensible proposals, not just for writing down proposals. I would like to point out that opposition parties have been in the government only recently, and all the good proposals you have made could have been implemented in previous years. There have always been objective and subjective reasons for not being able to do so. But I want to stress that from 2020 an operational budget will be introduced. This is part of the reform of the state, which will result in more effective and efficient implementation of the state's tasks, a higher quality of public service provision, a reduction in government expenditure and staffing, and more flexible and less bureaucratic organisation of government administration. More effective state governance requires the ability to make optimal use of available resources, which often also means implementing strategic objectives beyond the borders of ministries and organisations. In order to increase the efficiency of service provision, it is necessary to measure its cost, which in turn requires the linking of resources with operational information and better monitoring and evaluation of the outputs and results of activities. The implementation of the ABB concept will result in a system linking strategic and operational management levels and providing managers with the necessary financial and management information for decision-making, implementation, control and correction. As a result of the elimination of duplication of activities and the organisation of the cost structure, new development, investment and policy initiatives can be mobilised from existing resources. As a result of this change, we can move the subsequent budgetary debates from the question of how much money I get to the question of what can be done with this money. This will enable us, instead of base-based budgeting, to make budgeting on a zero base and to transform public services into the most beneficial and valuable for people. Let's look at next year's budget. After all, we have forecasted revenue for 2020 at EUR 11.8 billion and expenditure and investment at EUR 11.6 billion, of which EUR 1.05 billion will come from external support. The general government budget is in nominal balance and is moving towards structural balance. The structural deficit is 0.7% of GDP in 2020, 0.2% in 2021 and a structural balance should be achieved in 2022. At the same time, it should be seen that the government debt ratio is decreasing both in euro and in terms of the share of GDP. ParlaMint-EE_2019-11-13_U436	Now we can move on to today's item No 5, which is the second reading of § 61 of the Act on Supplementation to State Pension Insurance, initiated by the Government of the Republic. Mr Möldri, Chairman of the Social Affairs Committee of the Speaker's Committee, would you like a presentation?
ParlaMint-EE_2019-11-13_U437	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, The Social Affairs Committee of the Riigikogu discussed the draft Act supplementing § 61 of the State Pension Insurance Act initiated by the Government of the Republic in preparation for the second reading at its meeting on 4 November. The substance of the draft is, in short, that we want to increase pensions by an additional EUR 7 from 1 April 2020 after indexation. Representatives of the Ministry of Finance and the Ministry of Social Affairs also attended the meeting. The first reading of the draft was in the large hall on 15 October. The deadline for amendments was 29 October. An amendment was tabled by the Group of the Estonian Reform Party and its opinion on the draft was also expressed by the Association of Estonian Pensioners. The Association of Associations of Estonian Pensioners approves the draft law initiated by the Government of the Republic. The aim of the amendment of the Estonian Reform Party is to make Estonia's average old-age pension exempt from income tax and also to change the title of the bill by adding an amendment to the Income Tax Act. In the debate that took place in the Commission, we focused in particular on this amendment. Members of the Commission were interested in the number of pensioners who have to pay income tax on their pensions after the implementation of this law. In 2020, 184000 pensioners' pensions are expected to be taxed, i.e. 55% of all pensioners. The total pension or pension and salary is expected to exceed EUR 6000 per year for 204700 pensioners, i.e. 61% of all pensioners. There were also questions about history: how old age pensions have been taxed in Estonia in general. The representative of the Ministry announced that it has been tried to keep this line so that the Estonian average pension would be tax-free. The current government coalition is also working on this, but the budgetary possibilities set their limits. In general, pensions have been taxed in very different ways since Estonia regained independence. It was also asked whether the coalition considered two alternatives: either to make the average pension exempt or to increase the pension exceptionally by EUR 7? The amount of money needed could be more or less in the same order of magnitude. In this regard, the representative of the Ministry of Finance noted that this had been a political choice. Two alternatives have indeed been considered: either an additional EUR 70 tax revenue for pensioners or an extraordinary EUR 7 pension increase. The additional tax-free income of EUR 50 would have an impact, in particular, on pensioners whose pension is more than EUR 500, but the current proposal of the Government of the Republic to raise the basic pension by EUR 7 affects primarily those whose pension is less than EUR 500. The Commission proposed to treat these amendments as a single package. There was a vote on the amendment in the committee, and the commission decided not to take it into account. In addition, by consensus, the following decisions were taken: to put the draft on the agenda of today's plenary session, to propose that the second reading be closed, to propose that the draft be sent to third reading when the second reading is closed, and then to hold a final vote.

I'm sure every manager has his own kinks, but I'm terribly upset when you guys live in this hall talking about issues that have nothing to do with today's presentation. Anyone who really wants to talk could go out and talk in the hallway. As legendary John Bercow said, every waitress is right to be heard. So let's keep going. Please, Mr. Minister!
ParlaMint-EE_2019-11-13_U494	Thank you. To begin with, I would like to talk at all about what these fundamental principles of criminal policy are all about. The need will be clear to everyone if we open any newspaper on any day. Not a day goes by when society does not talk about criminal policy, whether it is the length of criminal proceedings, the independence of the prosecutor's office, the termination of some proceedings, the release of someone, domestic violence, gangs of young people or something else. The Riigikogu approved the first fundamentals of criminal policy in 2003. This was one of the first development documents adopted by the Riigikogu, where political parties agreed on priorities when crossing party borders, which have been caught for 16 years. Writer Hainsalu, one of the restorers of our independence, has said that if there are more needs in life than there are opportunities, obligations more than can be done, fate seems unjust until it comes to realize that all chance attracts a person, chooses the tasks to be solved. This idea also applies more broadly to the state. As a country, too, we must make choices. I dare say that both the reform of the special schools, which transformed the existing penal institutions into educational institutions showing the direction of children, and the systematic action against school bullying began with developments in criminal policy. Of course, the Ministry of Education and Research, the School Free of Temptation and the hearty teachers and the good parents whose work we see the fruits of it must be acknowledged. The Riigikogu defined this problem as a priority in 2011. The same can be said of domestic violence. Let's look at the length of the procedure in juvenile matters. We know how important it is to react properly and quickly to the actions of minors. This thinking, too, emerged from the fundamentals of criminal policy. The number of offences committed by minors has decreased by three times, to about a thousand per year. Nearly two thirds of juvenile delinquency will be resolved in less than three months. And what is especially important: we are now taking a different view of children and young people. We do not tend to threaten and punish them first, but to determine what is best for them. It usually means working hard with a young person and his family. We have also developed a support service for those released from prison. Something like this did not exist in 2011, but now we are hoping for more and more benefits in reducing repeat crimes. I will give these examples because it would be obvious how important it is to set priorities in the Riigikogu. It's the only way that what we do has an effect. And it is equally important that the Riigikogu constantly reminds the government of these priorities and asks how the achievement of the objectives is progressing. It is up to the government to ensure that priorities are not ignored for lack of money. Thus, the fundamentals of criminal policy are needed in order for the state to act in a consistent and smart manner in preventing offences and to focus on the most important, because there is not enough resources and resources for everything. In a sense, the level of crime in Estonia has returned to normal. The situation is not comparable to the chaos of the 1990s, during which one person was killed every day. This is not even comparable to 16 years ago when Estonia approved accession to the European Union in a referendum. Since then, crime has been halved, so now we are registering some 27,000 crimes a year. Still, we're doing 40-to-50 killings a year. This puts us among the last ones in the European Union. We still have the anxiety that forces us to grab a knife in a vodka anger. And although the number of prisoners in Estonian prisons has almost doubled, there are still nearly 2,500 small towns. With this, too, we have written at the end of the European ranking. Fortunately, fewer and fewer people are victims of crime than twice as many as eight years ago. The proportion of people who feel safe has tripled, and only 15% of people feel unsafe around home after dark. But although 4/5 of the Estonian people feel safe, the country's most important task should remain to provide our people with security, freedom, openness, human focus. So what is the target we need to reach by 2030? More broadly, we have three problems in criminal policy and, consequently, we also offer three priorities. First priority: Estonian criminal justice system is effective, independent, human-centred and victim-friendly. This means that the system turns its face more towards a person. In the legal system, a person has the opportunity to communicate with a person, not with a system. And the offender must not only feel the unmistakable consequence of the machine, but first of all he must make amends to the victim, do everything to make up for the misdemeanor, including apologies. Money taken from offenders must reach the victims of crimes. Let the procedure be quick, it must not exceed a reasonable time, and also digital, without excessive bureaucracy. And the background and needs of each individual offender must be taken into account. This will lead to an effective procedure. Priority two: children and young people fall into the field of view of the criminal justice system in exceptional cases and young offenders are treated in the light of their needs. This means a joint effort with the family, municipality, education, culture, sport and social system, so that young people do not come to the criminal system. If these efforts at home and at school and through sport and city planning do not produce results, then everything will be done in our system so that it is not a prison that young people end up in, so that we have alternatives that are not yet available. Priority 3: Crime, including its reversibility, is low, the policy of punishment is effective. This means that addicts and mentally ill offenders receive treatment and support. At present, it is not being given enough, which is why these people make up the majority of repeat criminals. And in our form, in European prisons, the point is to direct the riders to do justice in society. Since prison rules make society reflect, it is normal for prisoners to work, participate in programmes, acquire education, etc. Overall, the goal is that there will be fewer violence and offenders in Estonia in 2030, the offence procedure will be effective and victim-friendly, and children and young people will rarely enter the criminal justice system. Good members of the Riigikogu! Finally, I would like to draw attention to a small but important nuance. The government reports to the Riigikogu every year on the implementation of the fundamental principles of criminal policy, where necessary with the involvement of the state's attorney general and the ministers involved in the subject. Please take this opportunity to ask questions to all concerned. We'd be happy to answer them. For you remember, security for the Estonian people is the most important task of the state. Criminal policy, along with many other national policies, serves security. We will keep what we have been able to build so far, focus on young people, a procedure that cares for victims and is effective, and give the opportunity to those offenders who can be redesigned! Thank you for your attention and I would like to have a meaningful and cost-effective debate in the future.

ParlaMint-EE_2019-11-13_U540	Honourable Vice-Chairman of the Riigikogu! Ladies and gentlemen, One of the biggest tasks of this hall is for Estonia to be the rule of law and for us to have legal certainty. Debate on developments in criminal policy is certainly one way to say which rule of law we want in the large Chamber of the Riigikogu. Of course, the vast majority have declarative provisions in this document, and perhaps that should be the case. But it is also important that the Riigikogu really signals that Estonia is the rule of law and that independence is undoubtedly a guarantee of trust in the criminal justice system, i.e. the courts, the prosecutor's office, the police, the prison system, as well as in criminal custody. Estonia is in a pretty high international position in this field. According to the World Bank index, as we can read in the explanatory memorandum, Estonia is higher than, for example, the US, which is, according to one government association, a role model, and much higher than neighbouring countries with a similar background. Similarly, Estonia has a very high position in terms of indicators that measure the independence of the judiciary. As I mentioned in my question, we are 10th in comparison to 126 evaluated countries. These are the values that have been created for almost 30 years, and these are the values that we need to keep. Every Estonian politician, whether acting at municipal level or at state level, whether a member of the parliament or government, must set an example, instill legal certainty for the Estonian people and confirm that the Estonian legal system is independent and remains independent. I was pleased to hear that the EKRE representatives in the government agreed with the document, as confirmed by the Minister for Justice, and the Chairman of the Committee on Legal Affairs also said that there were no differences of opinion. Consequently, the representatives of the EKRE did not comment that we had problems with the independence of the courts, the police or the prosecutor's office. I very much hope that what the Minister of Finance and the Minister of the Interior has said is their personal opinion, namely that it does not coincide with the current positions and perceptions of the Estonian state. But it is really very strange to hear it being said that the Estonian prosecutor's office is politically biased, but in a document where it would be possible to state at the level of the government that we do not agree with what is written here, we do not say anything. It's kind of a two-faced behavior. I also asked the Chancellor of Justice about the relevant guarantees of the Estonian justice system. And the Chancellor of Justice replied to me today, it is also printed in the pages, that we have created a system in Estonia that guarantees the independence of the criminal justice system. ParlaMint-EE_2019-11-13_U548	We are now going to agenda item 7 today, which is the first reading of the draft 53th reading of the Riigikogu decision 'The fundamentals of legislative policy until 2030' presented by the Government of the Republic. Minister of Justice Raivo Aegis, please waitress!
ParlaMint-EE_2019-11-13_U549	Honourable Chairman of the Riigikogu! Honourable members of the Riigikogu! Like the previous strategy paper, it has been prepared and presented by the Ministry of Justice through the Government of the Republic to the Riigikogu for discussion. These are the fundamentals of legislative policy until 2030. The fundamentals of legislative policy aim to create a good legislative standard and thus to be a long-term indicator of policy orientation on which legislation can be based. The law is and will remain the main instrument of political decision-making in the democratic rule of law. It is therefore crucial that legislation is agreed at the level of the legislator on how laws are created. The fundamentals are addressed both to the Government of the Republic and, in some ways, to the Riigikogu. Clear formulation and enforcement of policy bases are also important for stakeholders and the wider community to ensure the predictability and openness of legislative policy. The draft consists of two major parts. Firstly, the fundamentals agree on the principles of good lawmaking. Compared to developments in legal policy until 2018, the orientations taken remain largely unchanged in the fundamentals of legislative policy until 2030. It will continue to be central to achieving inclusive and evidence-based legislation based on good lawmaking principles. More clearly than in the past, the principle that legislation is not the first but the last means of resolving problems has been emphasised. Similarly, the new fundamentals, unlike so far, have set the objective of reducing the volume of legislation. New legislation will only be proposed if their necessity is clearly justified. The draft basic principles provide for the development of draft laws, the development of a legal language and legislative cooperation and development over the next ten years. The draft law initiated by the Government of the Republic must be drawn up in order to establish a plan for drafting the necessary legislation. Exemption from this is only permitted in the case of an argument of public interest which must be justified in the explanatory memorandum to the draft law. The aim of the fundamentals is to involve the Riigikogu in the early stage of legislative drafting. As an update, the Riigikogu will be informed of the new draft as soon as the intention to develop is submitted to the public debate. The Riigikogu can then decide whether it wishes to discuss the proposed changes with representatives of the government. The aim is for legislation to start in more cases from the planned phase of development, which will ensure a more thorough preparatory work and a better quality result. In a democratic rule of law, legislation must be open and transparent. To this end, target groups must be involved as early as possible and better placed to ensure transparency. The draft decision stresses that, in order to ensure the availability of legislation and the transparency of the legislative procedure, the IT solutions used must be citizen-centred, accessible and comprehensive to all. The use of available data has been stressed in the analysis of impacts. There is a lot of data in our e-state that can be used in impact analyses. The legislator will also be guided to consider the use of innovative methods for carrying out the necessary impact analyses. The draft principles call for more ex-post evaluation to be carried out to verify the achievement of the objectives set in the adoption of the law. In order to achieve a clearer legal language, the draft stresses that language training is necessary for all civil servants to have legal language training in mind. As an update, it is proposed that, in justified cases, the user will be tested for comprehension of the text at the time of drafting. The draft also highlights legislative cooperation and development activities. There is a direct link between the professional skills of law-making lawyers and legislative proposals of good legal quality. Every year, the Government of the Republic must inform the Riigikogu of the fulfilment of the fundamental principles. As an update, it is proposed that the government report be accompanied by an expert opinion, which should reflect an external assessment of what has been done. The decision would enter into force once it was signed in accordance with § 1121 of the Riigikogu Home and Working Procedures Act. Thank you for your attention.

ParlaMint-EE_2019-11-13_U583	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Good fellows! The Group of the Social Democratic Party notes that the document on the fundamentals of legislative policy contains some correct ideas. In many respects, however, the government does not behave according to the fundamentals. The aim of improving the quality of legislation is to be welcomed. We agree that legislation must be in simple language, clear and precise. Let's hope we get to that goal someday. At the same time, the draft is full of selective and strange restrictions. For example, according to the draft, the Government of the Republic must draw up an intention to draft any draft legislation it intends to draft, but it is added that the Government may not draw up it if it is justified by the public interest. That's what it says in the explanatory memorandum to the bill. It doesn't make any sense what public interest means. At present, the good lawmaking and regulatory rules established by the government set out specific points in which the intention to develop may not be drawn up. If there is a public interest in the future instead, all drafts may not be drawn up. Political will, for example, is behind the dismantling of the pension system. The impact assessment seeks data that fits the political will. The question arises where there is an intention to develop and where the public interest is that the intention to develop has been abandoned. I asked the Minister to comment on the dismantling of the second pillar of the pension without prior analysis of its effects. The Minister admitted in the committee that members of the Riigikogu do not have the opportunity to analyse the effects on the same scale as the Ministry can. This suggests that members of the Riigikogu could be assisted. Consequently, the Chancellery of the Riigikogu needs much more money. Time also pointed out that the draft on the second pillar of the pension was not yet ready, and he said he believed that the draft would be accompanied by an impact analysis. However, I would like to point out that the impact will still be assessed before the draft is drawn up. According to the Minister, the fundamentals are an idealised approach to the legislative process. But sometimes it is not rational to design a plan with impact analysis, because the change is completely understandable without it. The best legislation can be born if it is based on reason. This is how the question arises, which is why we need a document on the fundamentals of legislative policy. Developments in legislative policy require that the Riigikogu does not process the draft if it does not have the intention to develop it. Drafts initiated or submitted in the Riigikogu are not yet required to be drawn up. According to the Riigikogu's Home and Rules of Procedure Act, the Riigikogu must decide on the adoption of the draft procedure within three working days. At the same time, the Riigikogu Board has no analytical centre required for this purpose and therefore has no competence to reject the draft on this basis. The imposition of such an obligation limits the right of a Member to initiate a draft or to propose amendments and is therefore incompatible with the Constitution. This directly violates the right of members of the Riigikogu, political groups and committees to initiate draft laws. The fundamentals foresee that the impact of the amendment to the draft of the Riigikogu will be assessed in the course of the procedure, and the explanatory memorandum states that Parliament has the necessary resources for this purpose in the form of the Development Monitoring Centre and the Legal and Analytical Department. However, the work of the Development Monitoring Centre is regulated by law and, if they were to undertake an analysis of the effects of the drafts, they would violate existing law. In the Legal and Analytical Department, as we know, only four people with analyst backgrounds are working and meeting this requirement would require an increase in the number of legal and analytical staff. Head of the Legal and Analytical Department of the Chancellery of the Riigikogu, Antero Habicht, acting as Deputy Director, stated at the meeting of the committee... Three more minutes, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2019-11-13_U584	Three more minutes, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2019-11-13_U585	... that the amendment to the State Budget Act is mostly accompanied by a large number of amendments, sometimes even several hundred. The deadline for the submission of proposals will normally be 10 days and the effects of the changes should also be assessed within that period. The problem would be the lack of basic data, as primary data are often used internally by ministries. It is therefore very difficult to create the capacity of the Chancellery of the Riigikogu, as is currently the case in all ministries. The draft concerns the assessment of the impact of the proposed amendments by stating that the impact that the implementation of the law may entail will be assessed before the draft is drawn up on the basis of the principle of proportionality. The impact assessment of a change with significant impact shall be more comprehensive and shall include, inter alia, an analysis of other possible solutions. The Riigikogu ensures that the impact of the amendment to the draft of the Riigikogu during the procedure is also assessed. According to developments in legislative policy, the amendment must include a reference to the part of the draft which is to be amended and recommend the exact wording of the amendment. In view of this, the obligation to extend such an impact analysis to all amendments creates a procedure in which the Commission can skip an amendment in which a member of the Riigikogu, a political group or a committee has failed to provide alternatives in an important impact analysis. Such a requirement for an impact analysis infringes the right of an ambassador, political group and committee under the Constitution to initiate drafts and propose amendments. It is also clear that a member of the Riigikogu does not have the same means of ordering impact analyses as the government has. If the lead committee of the draft proposal were to decide whether the amendment had been subject to the necessary impact assessment, it might happen that the majority of the commission considered that there was no necessary impact assessment and that the amendment could be rejected. This requirement would certainly restrict the opposition's ability to exercise its constitutional rights. It also fails to understand the place where it is specifically emphasised that the principles of good lawmaking must also be applied within the first hundred days of the government. And why is such a document indicating a coalition agreement? Finally, the Social Democrats are of the opinion that the right of members of the Riigikogu to initiate and amend drafts must not be restricted in order to improve the quality of legislation. The imposition of excessively strict requirements on the draft law and its amendments restricts, in particular, the opposition's ability to exercise its constitutional rights. The main aim of the document on the fundamentals of legislative policy as a vision document is essentially that the Riigikogu gives the Government of the Republic specific long-term guidelines for the implementation of better lawmaking policy. We have presented our proposals and opinions to the Committee on Legal Affairs. We will certainly also table specific amendments. In the form it is today, it is certainly not possible to support this draft. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2019-11-13_U594	Mr President-in-Office of the Council. Honourable Riigikogu! The draft was discussed by the Finance Committee on 4 November and presented by Minister of Finance Martin Helme, previous speaker, and Anneli Valgma, chief specialist at the Tax Policy Department of the Ministry of Finance. Indeed, the draft concerns the exchange of information on schemes affecting taxation, the exchange of information on financial accounts and the identification of the beneficial owner. And as the previous speaker said, the draft aims to reduce aggressive tax planning and increase the transparency of national tax systems. I am not going to describe the substance of the draft, the Minister has already described it. I would like to examine briefly the issues and questions that we had in the Commission under discussion. Indeed, the Minister explained the purpose and content of this draft and the Members of the Commission also asked questions. The chairman of the committee was first interested in which draft payment concerns. The Chief Specialist of the Tax Policy Department of the Ministry of Finance, who answered all the questions, explained that the Directive regulates the exchange of information on direct taxes. He also stressed that the main risk was that profits would be transferred to the country where they were exempt but where they could be deducted. There you go. They then asked whether the bill was also about price manipulation. Indeed, the answer was that the scheme criteria, together with the explanations, were included in the draft decree of the Ministry of Finance. It was then asked who would be subject to regulatory reporting obligations. The Chief Specialist of the Ministry of Finance explained that the aim is primarily to catch large corporations that have developed and implement the schemes. It was also clarified whether the regulation concerns companies of all sizes. The answer was that in principle this is the case, regulation does not distinguish companies by size. Countries exchange quite a lot of information among themselves and it is possible to improve regulation when new schemes appear. The speaker asked that, since the directive also covers schemes that can avoid the exchange of financial accounts, whether the draft concerns money laundering. The answer of the Chief Specialist of the Ministry of Finance was that regulation is also linked to money laundering. The Organisation for Economic Cooperation and Development (OECD) developed measures to protect the exchange of financial accounts and schemes for hiding the beneficial owner, and this part came from the OECD. The speaker was also interested in what would happen if companies were to circumvent bank questionnaires. The answer was that tax advisors could indeed recommend schemes that allow money to be transferred from a bank to a place that works like a bank, but is essentially not a bank. For example, it may be recommended to place money in an image that is similar to a bank account but does not meet the requirements of the bank account information exchange obligation, or to keep money in a country that does not exchange such information at all. Well, then they asked if this bill had anything to do with crypto money. The answer was that it could be interpreted as such and that it could also be interpreted as meaning that crypto assets are covered by this regulation. It was then intended to clarify whether this draft really concerned only the transposition of the directive, and the answer was that, yes, this draft concerns only the transposition of the directive. The speaker also asked whether temporary agency workers' schemes also fell within the scope of this draft. To that end, the answer was yes: they are. The committee chairman then asked what would happen if this law were not passed. The answer was that, in this case, it is an infringement procedure of the European Union and fines can be quite high, because it is an issue of tax transparency, which is why the issue is a matter of particular concern at European level. By the way, the proposal for a directive was already tabled during the Estonian Presidency of the European Union. Finally, it was asked whether regulation could also be used, for example, to restrict competition and to block the practice of optimising the taxes that we accept. The answer was that the directive concerns, above all, the risk of aggressive tax planning, and we do not face much danger from this point of view. Thank you for your attention. I'll answer the questions.

ParlaMint-EE_2019-11-13_U596	Dear Speaker! Honourable members of the Riigikogu! We come before you with a law amending the Tax Exchange Act. This law transposes the directive by which we will exchange information on tax schemes that affect taxation, bank account information and the identification of the beneficial owner. The aim of extending the exchange of information is to combat aggressive tax planning and to obtain more information on possible gaps in their tax laws. The reporting obligation is a scheme which has a link to any foreign country and whose characteristics meet one of the criteria set out exhaustively in the Finance Minister's Regulation. Aggressive tax planning cannot be defined with unchanged characteristics. Therefore, at the level of the Regulation, criteria that may indicate aggressive tax planning or transactions with which to hide its assets are laid down. All schemes that meet these characteristics must be submitted to the tax authorities. The main interest is to obtain information on aggressive tax planning schemes that abuse gaps between national tax laws. In particular, tax relief or tax avoidance schemes are targeted. Information must be provided primarily by advisors operating in Estonia who have developed the scheme. The information on the scheme ordered or self-developed from a third country shall be provided by the taxpayer itself, even if the lawyer or auditor who developed the scheme cannot provide information due to the protection of professional secrecy arising from the law. The customer may also exempt the lawyer from the obligation of professional secrecy at any time. The right of non-incriminating self-incrimination, which protects the person providing the information, is included in the draft act, so as not to disclose information which would incriminate him or his close identity in committing a misdemeanour or a criminal offence. The scheme shall be submitted to the Tax and Customs Board within 30 calendar days of the transfer to the customer or of the start of the implementation of the scheme. The tax and customs office shall send the information collected to the central register maintained by the European Commission through which Member States shall have access to information concerning them. For the first time, we must send information on the schemes in October next year. The draft does not take over the norm according to which the tax authorities may also require taxable persons to report on the use of the scheme. Where information on the sale or implementation of schemes is provided to the tax authorities, further information on their subsequent use does not have sufficient significant added value. This law is planned to enter into force on 1 January 2020. It shall apply from 1 July of the following year. That's all for me. I'll answer the questions.

ParlaMint-EE_2019-11-13_U598	Dear Deputy Speaker! Happy Members of the Riigikogu! I will present to you the country's consolidated accounts for the financial year 2018. This report gives you an overview of the steps taken by the government in 2018 with taxpayers' money to boost our economy, people's well-being and security, and as a result our country's financial situation changed. Overall, we are moving in the right direction: the economy grew by 4.8%, people are doing better, the average wage increased by 7.6%, the number of people employed increased by 0.9% and unemployment decreased to 5.4%. Now, in a comprehensive way, the government's goals have been achieved. One of the general objectives was to increase Estonia's population in 2018 and it grew by almost 5,000 people, reaching 1323824 people. However, the total birth rate did not increase at the expected rate. The second objective was to increase social well-being and cohesion. The rate of relative poverty has unfortunately not decreased very quickly in recent years; work must continue in this direction, and this is what the current government is doing. At the same time, the number of people with strong national identity has increased. The aim of the Government of the Republic was also to remove Estonia from slow economic growth. In order to meet this objective, we will assess the productivity rate per employed person, with an observed growth trend, but it could grow faster. At the same time, more older people have been able to enter the labour market. When speaking about the economic environment in general, it can be said that by 2018, the Estonian economy had recovered in many respects from the global crisis that began ten years earlier. Both the substantial strengthening of external demand and the strong increase in public investment in 2017 contributed to this. As the Estonian economy had returned to its potential level at the time of the preparation of the state budget for 2018 and employment created historical records, the government increased its investments. However, as a hindsight, we can see that the Estonian economy was overheated by then and that the additional stimulus from the state was not directly necessary. Last but certainly not least, the objective of the government in 2018 was to maintain and strengthen national security. In this area, too, we have achieved our goal. Despite rapid economic growth, the share of military defence costs, together with the host country's costs, is more than 2% of GDP as in previous years. The summary report for the financial year of the State has been compiled on the basis of the accounts of all public sector entities. It shall consist of an annual report and financial statements and shall include data on the state, local government, government and public sector as a whole. As of 31 December 2018, the general government had assets worth a total of EUR 17.08 billion, most of which were fixed assets with an exact value of EUR 13.39 billion. Compared to the previous period, the value of the assets increased by EUR 566.2 million. The total liabilities amounted to EUR 6,23 billion and increased by EUR 487.3 million compared to the previous period. The loan commitment decreased by 31.5 million in 2018 and amounted to 2.02 billion euros at the end of the year. The stabilisation reserve remained in the same order of magnitude as at the end of the previous period and amounted to EUR 411.5 million. To be exact, it decreased by 0.2% compared to the previous year. The liquidity reserve decreased by 9%, reaching EUR 676.6 million by the end of the year. Government revenue amounted to EUR 9.89 billion in 2018 and increased by 857.5 million, or 9.5%, compared to 2017. Of the revenues, the sum of social tax and social security contributions increased the most by 320.4 million euros and the increase by 12.1% and VAT by 125.8 million euros and the increase by 6.9%. Government expenditure amounted to EUR 9.72 billion, an increase of EUR 988.7 million or 11,3% over the year. Of the costs, the biggest increase was the grants granted, i.e. EUR 543.3 million, with an increase of 13.4%. These included benefits for the unemployed and work capacity benefits, which increased by EUR 118.6 million or 138.9%, and family allowances by EUR 79.2 million and an increase by 17%. Investments were made by the government sector in 2018 for a total of EUR 978 million. This is EUR 54.5 million more than in 2017 and growth is 5.9%. The nominal general government balance was in deficit in 2018 at 0.6% of GDP, or 146 million euros. Of this, the deficit of the central government was 1% of GDP and was partly offset by the surplus of local governments and social security funds, the first plus 0.2% and the second by + 0.3% of GDP. The deficit of the central government was mainly due to the strong increase in investment activity, with an increase of 15%, an increase of 36% in payments to the EU budget and an increase of 22% in operating expenses. On the other hand, interest and owner's income decreased by 23%. The results of the social security funds exceeded expectations for both the health insurance fund and the unemployment fund, due to the very good income from social tax and unemployment insurance contributions. The surplus of local governments was also mainly due to strong income tax receipts. The summary report of the financial year also passes the audit of the National Audit Office before submitting it to the Riigikogu. The National Audit Office found that the annual accounts for 2018 reflect the state's financial position in all material respects and that the financial results and cash flows of the reporting period ended fairly. According to the National Audit Office's audit report, the state's economic transactions have been carried out in a substantial part in accordance with the State Budget Act and the State Budget Act of 2018 and the Amending Act. As in previous years, the National Audit Office made proposals in its report on the financial management and internal control systems of the state. The ministers concerned mostly agreed with the recommendations made and promised to take them into account in their further activities or explained more specific circumstances to the National Audit Office.
