ParlaMint-EE_2019-11-13_U621	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Happy Members of the Riigikogu! Have a good late night. The National Audit Office sends several dozen reports to the parliament each year, two of which are here today for discussion by the Riigikogu. The first is the audit of the state's annual report, which has just been mentioned by the honourable Minister of Finance. This report relates to public money and other assets. And the second is the annual report on the major problems in the economy and administration of the country. Looking together and complementing each other, these reports provide Parliament with an overview of the use and preservation of state assets. This year's annual report, which I presented to the President of the Riigikogu the day before yesterday, focuses on the health and vitality of the Estonian e-governance. Why such a thing? But because Estonia has made a name for itself in the world as an e-governance. Estonia was one of the first to make the so-called tiger jump in government and public services and successfully went to the digital world. Our e-Governance success history is all about being the mythical Estonian Nokia that has long been sought out. But today, unfortunately, no one wants to be Nokia anymore because it has become a quiet place. History doesn't matter when it comes to development. On the contrary. Famous history and success can feed satisfaction and harmony, but eventually bring stagnation. Today, only those countries that avoid entanglement in technology that has lived up to its own time can sustain success. Life shows that if IT solutions are not constantly updated, information systems will eventually dictate political choices. I believe that perhaps many of you have seen situations in which the necessary decisions are postponed when dealing with the drafts, precisely because it is not possible to make the changes needed to implement the decision with reasonable speed and reasonable money in the information systems. Yes, at times such situations can prevent unnecessary haste, but by nature they hinder political processes. With a certain exaggeration, this situation is also a threat to democracy. A lot has been said about tiger jumping. Tiger is a territorial animal. He knows where he is right now. And our e-Tiger should know that too. Our e-governance is a source of national pride, but unfortunately our e-tiger is somewhat at a standstill. He doesn't know where he is and how big his litter is. Perhaps we do not know what this pride will cost us and it is difficult to get a clear picture of how many and which information systems and databases the Estonian state has. When preparing the annual review, the National Audit Office was interested in the amount of IT investments, as well as the administrative and personnel costs associated with IT. It turned out, however, that there was no such comprehensive review. It was easier to get information about how much money the ministries estimate is missing in IT. Therefore, the Ministry of Finance, together with the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications, should first establish an overview of the actual state of IT expenditure. In the political world, it is, as we know, very tempting to say that the economic costs of public authorities are being cut again. It brings popularity. However, since 2003, the administrative costs of IT systems have been integrated into the normal economic costs of public authorities. And that means that it is often those IT activities that are necessary to keep the systems running that are under pressure, because maintenance cannot be presented as a bright new job. Given the importance of the IT sector, it would be worth considering the possibility of separate accounting for IT maintenance costs and IT investments. If we define very precisely what is and what is not the cost of protection, the same definition of IT costs could be justified in every way. Since the National Audit Office did not find information about the IT costs of the central government, we made an attempt to compile such an overview ourselves. This was only partially successful, but the data were still sufficient to detect cost dynamics. It turned out that in the IT sector, human and administrative expenditure has increased by about 1/3 over the last two years. True, this is mainly due to the increase in the number of IT workers, not their wage increases. In this situation, the growth of private sector wages cannot be countered. In this way, a number of IT offices in public authorities have not been filled and the turnover of the workforce is high. At the same time, an increase in costs is inevitable if we want the e-state to develop and the existing information systems to work. Ladies and gentlemen, In the case of new IT developments, the government has used first and foremost the European Union contribution. However, it cannot be used to cover maintenance costs for systems already in operation. Estonia's own tax money is scarce. Similarly, the organisation and maintenance of information systems are not attractive or visible enough compared to the creation of new information systems. Investment in a visible facade brings more attention to everyone than, for example, replacing a decomposing electrical system. Here again the old problem emerges: the desire to constantly offer new and shiny things in a situation where essential basic functions have been overlooked. As an endless circle, new buildings will emerge between the ruins, which will soon be among the ruins themselves. The more we rely on the digital state, the more we must also contribute to the maintenance of the digital tiger. If there were a zoologist Alexei Turovski standing here instead of me, he probably wouldn't leave it unsaid that a tiger spends nearly half his waking time caring for his coat. Otherwise, his health, his capacity for jumping, and his smell would betray him before he reached the target. So is the e-ticket. We can't get away with extra spending if we're gonna keep the tiger jumping. Money is needed both to make current e-services work and to upgrade outdated IT solutions and to introduce new technologies at an optimal pace. In order to create a systematic plan for investments, the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications should be given a clearer leadership role, also in terms of monitoring and technological planning of IT developments. Good listeners! One of the components of Estonian e-story that attracted attention is the e-health system. The digital prescription solution and the patient portal are a big step forward. But the e-health project is controversial, shiny inside. The national digital registration office, which was due to be operational in 2008, was only operational 11 years later, in August this year. While the original application of the digital registration office was completed and accepted, the money was spent, but it was not used. And now a new information system had to be developed. Or we could look at a subject such as the ease of use of the eHealth system. Instead of helping patients, doctors need to spend an unreasonable amount of time clicking to match the medical records and get an overview of the patient's condition. A total of 65% of hospitals who responded to the National Audit Office survey found the use of the e-health system uncomfortable. 45% of respondents indicated that they do not receive all the data needed for treatment from the eHealth system. For example, when an ambulance is put on the road to the E.M.O. doctors with information about the patient, there are 24 pages from which to search for the necessary information in a stressful situation. Only four years ago, Estonia's e-health strategy was approved with new and ambitious goals. At the same time, earlier objectives remain unachievable. The question arises as to why, in an area of such importance, the problems known for a long time have not been solved. Why is it that, with the help of political and administrative will, certain issues are quickly triumphed, some problems being solved in the bird wing and in defiance, but that is not the case with other important things? In the case of eHealth, this is not so much an IT problem as a matter of long-term under-management and lack of ownership. Dear listeners, It is true that increasing digitalisation of everyday life will help make services more accessible, information collection faster and systems more efficient. But, unfortunately, it also has its own bad side: it provides a more effective opportunity to escalate stupidity and bureaucracy. In the digital world, it is much easier to ask for information that is not necessarily needed. In this way, an unnoticed click by click will be possible to burden people and organizations more and more in vain. In the near future, the National Audit Office will receive a report in which we analysed the collection of data on welfare. For example, the audit revealed that the Ministry of Social Affairs has instructed municipalities to submit 11 welfare reports on a regular basis in the S-web environment. In total, they have 793 data fields. Municipalities and cities do not need such detailed data themselves, nor do the usage examples provided by the Ministry of Social Affairs itself point to the need for one-off use of many data. For example, it has requested data which have been used only to prepare ministerial visits or to write articles. The Ministry of Social Affairs should urgently exempt municipalities from pointless collections of data. But not only is the Ministry of Social Affairs overburdening the municipalities, but many municipalities are not indebted to them, in turn, to those in need. They ask for information that is already available elsewhere or that is not actually used to provide a service. If, for example, 17 cells were sufficient in one municipality to receive income support, the second was already three times longer, a form with 50 cells. I will give you another example of the convenient possibility of increasing bureaucracy created by the e-solution. A few months ago, an article could be read on the Estonian National Broadcasting Portal, where the Head of the Procurement Department of the University of Tartu described the organisation of mini competitions before and after the development of the Public Procurement Register. In the past, asking for a quote for a mini competition meant that a simple e-mail was sent out, but now the same work has to be done in the respective information system. In the new situation, according to the university, it takes 100 clicks and 50 minutes to organise one mini competition. If the university makes on average nine daily price requests for travel services, this means that the university should employ a full-time clicker to process the price offers of travel services alone. The article also acknowledged the existence of a problem, but added that the Ministry did not see a quick solution. Let them click! Unfortunately, many of the ridiculous bureaucratic restrictions and requirements under which we groan are branded 'Made in Estonia'. If only there was time, we'd talk more about procurement. The National Audit Office is currently carrying out an audit on this subject. For example, two years ago, a new Public Procurement Act came into force, one of the objectives of which was to simplify public procurement procedures. In the course of the audit, it became clear that several authorities do not see this simplification anywhere. It is true that the Ministry of Finance has today started to bring together proposals from the authorities on how to simplify the amended law again. Unfortunately, the draft simplification will not be allowed to reach the Riigikogu until next year. The question arises as to why, for example, this law, which is a burden on the public sector, could not be changed at a speed worthy of the information society, such as the five-day deadline for coordination in the course of agile legislation. Honourable members of the Riigikogu! As I mentioned at the beginning of today's presentation, an assessment of the accounts for the previous year is also a traditional part of my review. And in the same traditional way, I can confirm that the country's annual accounts reflect the state's financial position and that the economic result and cash flows of the reporting period that ended are fairly to a significant extent. And just as my predecessors have done in the office of Auditor General on two occasions, Mihkel Oviir and Alar Karis on three occasions, I cannot fail to ask a similar question that Riina Sikkut just asked: what is the benefit of the 2018 summary report on 13 November 2019 when you are about to adopt the state budget for 2020 in this Chamber? Sure, if you do. For at least 15 years, the National Audit Office has broken its head on such a question. Just before the end of the current year, the annual report for the previous year has been approved by the Riigikogu for several decades, both before and during the e-state. In Sweden, however, the financial report is already presented to Parliament in April, Finland somewhat later. However, in both cases, this report can be used to prepare the state budget for next year. In Estonia, perhaps we could discuss what the content of this report should be, so that it would also be of practical benefit to anyone other than future accounting archaeologists. In addition, I think that it is possible to say in a much simpler and clearer way in the summary report of the national financial year what the money was spent on and what it was received for, and what was not achieved and why, really, by providing only the most important information. It should also be considered whether an annual report is needed in this form today. After all, technological solutions would allow the country's economic indicators to be constantly displayed in online mode and to be taken at all times as "pictures" of them. Honourable Riigikogu! Finally, a few words on the state budget. In recent times, experts in the Ministry of Finance have been in the habit of saying calmly about almost all issues and problems relating to budget and reporting that all concerns are solved by activity-based budgeting. But it won't. On the contrary. The annual state budget is increasingly less understandable and informative from year to year. This has been complained to the National Audit Office over the years by many members of the Riigikogu, regardless of whether they are currently in opposition or coalition. The activity-based budgeting, which has been fully operational for next year's state budget, has, on the other hand, provided a completely new opportunity to make the document even more complex. However, the problem is not the activity-based budgeting, where information is presented by programme. Whatever the budget is based on, it must be possible to present it, especially in the digital age. The government has decided to carry out a state budget review. In the course of this review, it would also be reasonable to carry out an audit of the state budget document and the annual report. If decision-makers and the public are able to understand the central document guiding the country's strategic development more easily, this will also provide an opportunity to make better savings decisions. I brought here an idea from spring this year. The current government took office on 29 April, just one month and two days before the deadline for the submission of the country's budgetary strategy as provided for by law. The strategy was submitted within the deadline. Could've been worse. If the government had taken office a month later, for example, because of longer coalition negotiations, then there would have been two days left. It didn't happen this time, but the next few times might happen. In the interests of the quality of financial planning, I think it would be reasonable, in the light of the future, to allow greater flexibility to agree on a minimum period of time from the government to the presentation of a budget strategy, even one month, so that the government can have a minimum time for substantive debates. In addition, we must note that this year, in the process of budgeting, we have set ourselves a goal, for some reason, of new heights to be heroically overcome. The law now provides that in the same timetable as the budgetary strategy, i.e. spring, the government will also have to approve the draft budget for next year. That's the draft, not the draft, as usual. Is it reasonable to make two draft national budgets a year, one in the spring and the other in the autumn? Why is it necessary to work in such detail before the summer economic forecast with the state budget law, as we all know that the spring document cannot be credible and the actual budget will be completed in autumn? To sum it up. The e-star in front of e-Estonia should not only mean electronic Estonia, but it could also mean effective Estonia, Estonia with goals and energetic Estonia. In many cases, digitisation and legislative changes are not needed to combat unnecessary use. Our northern neighbours have proven that there is simply a need for will and action. Just as we are, the Finns are struggling with bureaucracy. That is why the Finnish Government set itself the goal of cutting red tape a few years ago. As they were struggling with the processing of major projects, they undertook a project of real life as a practical test of implementing zero bureaucracy. This is why Äänikosk was involved in planning and licensing processes related to the large industry. As a result, the whole process was completed within nine months. During this period two detailed plans were carried out, environmental impact assessments were carried out and tens of environmental permits were processed, including building permits. And all of that without any change in any law. So the key to success was the will to solve the problem. This experience is now being applied to other similar processes. In Estonia, in order to reduce red tape, we first started to explain the requirements of legislation and to deal with changes to it, while the Finns proved that it was not the legislation that needs to be changed but the attitude to succeed. This attitude and will also serve us in the distant future when no one remembers what Nokia was and why they were looking for him in Estonia. Thanks!
